</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Mary Teresa Bojaxhiu</b>, MC (<small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[bɔjaˈdʒiu]</a></span>; 26 August 1910&#160;– 5 September 1997), better known as <b>Mother Teresa</b> (<a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a>: <i lang="sq">Nënë Tereza</i>), was an Albanian-Indian <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a> <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nun</a> who, in 1950, founded the <a href="/wiki/Missionaries_of_Charity" title="Missionaries of Charity">Missionaries of Charity</a>. <b>Anjezë Gonxhe Bojaxhiu</b> (<small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[aˈɲɛzə ˈɡɔndʒɛ bɔjaˈdʒiu]</a></span>) was born in  <a href="/wiki/Skopje" title="Skopje">Skopje</a>—at the time, part of the Ottoman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> After eighteen years, she moved to Ireland and then to India, where she lived most of her life. <b>Saint Teresa of Calcutta</b><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a> on 4 September 2016. The anniversary of her death is her <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a>.
</p><p>After Mother Teresa founded her <a href="/wiki/Religious_congregation" title="Religious congregation">religious congregation</a>, it grew to have over 4,500 nuns and was active in 133 countries as of 2012<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mother_Teresa&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The congregation manages homes for people who are dying of <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS" title="HIV/AIDS">HIV/AIDS</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leprosy" title="Leprosy">leprosy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a>. The congregation also runs <a href="/wiki/Soup_kitchen" title="Soup kitchen">soup kitchens</a>, dispensaries, mobile clinics, children's and family counselling programmes, as well as <a href="/wiki/Orphanage" title="Orphanage">orphanages</a> and schools. Members take vows of <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_counsels" title="Evangelical counsels">chastity, poverty, and obedience</a> and also profess a fourth vow: to give "wholehearted free service to the poorest of the poor."<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mother Teresa received several honours, including the 1962 <a href="/wiki/List_of_Ramon_Magsaysay_Award_winners" title="List of Ramon Magsaysay Award winners">Ramon Magsaysay Peace Prize</a> and the 1979 <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>. A <a href="/wiki/Criticism_of_Mother_Teresa" title="Criticism of Mother Teresa">controversial figure</a> during her life and after her death, Mother Teresa was admired by many for her charitable work. She was praised and criticised on various counts, such as for her views on abortion and contraception, and was criticized for poor conditions in her houses for the dying. Her authorized biography was written by <a href="/wiki/Navin_Chawla" title="Navin Chawla">Navin Chawla</a> and published in 1992, and she has been the subject of <a href="#Film_and_literature">other books as well as films</a>. On 6 September 2017, Mother Teresa and Saint <a href="/wiki/Francis_Xavier" title="Francis Xavier">Francis Xavier</a> were named co-patrons of the Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Calcutta.
</p>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:lavender;text-align:center;">Denominations</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content"><div class="plainlist" style="padding-top:0.3em;">
<p><u><b><a href="/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christian_denominations" title="Saint Thomas Christian denominations">Saint Thomas Christian denominations</a></b></u><br />
</p>

</p><p><u><b><a href="/wiki/Protestantism_in_India" title="Protestantism in India">Protestant denominations</a></b></u><br />
<a href="/wiki/Andhra_Evangelical_Lutheran_Church" title="Andhra Evangelical Lutheran Church">Andhra Evangelical Lutheran</a>, <a href="/wiki/Assemblies_Jehovah_Shammah" title="Assemblies Jehovah Shammah">Assemblies Jehovah Shammah</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christian_Revival_Church" title="Christian Revival Church">Christian Revival Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_North_India" title="Church of North India">Church of North India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_South_India" title="Church of South India">Church of South India</a>, <a href="/wiki/Garo_Baptist_Convention" title="Garo Baptist Convention">Garo Baptist</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_Brethren" title="Indian Brethren">Indian Brethren</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_Pentecostal_Church_of_God" title="Indian Pentecostal Church of God">Indian Pentecostal Church of God</a>, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_God_(Full_Gospel)_in_India" title="Church of God (Full Gospel) in India">Church of God (Full Gospel)</a>, <a href="/wiki/North_Bank_Baptist_Christian_Association" title="North Bank Baptist Christian Association">North Bank Baptist Christian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northern_Evangelical_Lutheran_Church" title="Northern Evangelical Lutheran Church">Northern Evangelical Lutheran</a>, <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Church_in_India" title="Methodist Church in India">Methodist Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_of_India" title="Presbyterian Church of India">Presbyterian</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_Pentecostal_Mission" title="The Pentecostal Mission">The Pentecostal Mission</a>, <a href="/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist_Church" title="Seventh-day Adventist Church">Seventh-day Adventist</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_Evangelical_Lutheran_Churches_in_India" title="United Evangelical Lutheran Churches in India">United Evangelical Lutheran</a>
</p>
</div></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:lavender;text-align:center;">Organisations</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christianity_in_India_sidebar" title="Template:Christianity in India sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christianity_in_India_sidebar" title="Template talk:Christianity in India sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christianity_in_India_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Urban stone-and-glass building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg/220px-Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg/330px-Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg/440px-Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="532" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Memorial_house_of_Mother_Teresa.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Memorial_House_of_Mother_Teresa" title="Memorial House of Mother Teresa">Memorial House of Mother Teresa</a> in her native Skopje</div></div></div>
<p>Mother Teresa's given name was Anjezë Gonxhe (or Gonxha)<sup id="cite_ref-Egan_1992_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Egan_1992-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (September 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Bojaxhiu (<small>Albanian:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sq-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Albanian" title="Help:IPA/Albanian">[aˈɲɛzə ˈɡɔndʒɛ bɔjaˈdʒiu]</a></span>—<span title="Albanian-language text"><i lang="sq">Anjezë</i></span> is a <a href="/wiki/Cognate" title="Cognate">cognate</a> of "Agnes"; <span title="Albanian-language text"><i lang="sq">Gonxhe</i></span> means "rosebud" or "little flower" in <a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a>. She was born on 26 August 1910 into a <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_Albanians" title="Kosovo Albanians">Kosovar Albanian</a> family<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Skopje" title="Skopje">Skopje</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> (now the capital of <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia" title="North Macedonia">North Macedonia</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-MToC_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MToC-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> She was baptised in Skopje the day after her birth.<sup id="cite_ref-Egan_1992_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Egan_1992-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> She later considered 27 August, the day she was baptised, her "true birthday".<sup id="cite_ref-MToC_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MToC-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She was the youngest child of <a href="/wiki/Nikoll%C3%AB_Bojaxhiu" title="Nikollë Bojaxhiu">Nikollë</a> and Dranafile Bojaxhiu (Bernai).<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Her father, who was involved in <a href="/wiki/Albanians_in_North_Macedonia" title="Albanians in North Macedonia">Albanian-community</a> politics in <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia_under_the_Ottoman_Empire" title="North Macedonia under the Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Macedonia</a>, died in 1919 when she was eight years old.<sup id="cite_ref-MToC_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MToC-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> He was born in <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a> (today in <a href="/wiki/Kosovo" title="Kosovo">Kosovo</a>), however, his family was from <a href="/wiki/Mirdita" title="Mirdita">Mirdita</a> (present-day <a href="/wiki/Albania" title="Albania">Albania</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-Lolja_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lolja-18">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Mehmeti_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mehmeti-19">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Her mother may have been from a village near <a href="/wiki/Gjakova" title="Gjakova">Gjakova</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-origin_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origin-20">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> believed by her offspring to be <a href="/wiki/Bishtazhin" title="Bishtazhin">Bishtazhin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a biography by Joan Graff Clucas, Anjezë was in her early years when she became fascinated by stories of the lives of <a href="/wiki/Missionaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Missionaries">missionaries</a> and their service in <a href="/wiki/Bengal" title="Bengal">Bengal</a>; by age 12, she was convinced that she should commit herself to religious life.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Her resolve strengthened on 15 August 1928 as she prayed at the shrine of the <a href="/wiki/Black_Madonna" title="Black Madonna">Black Madonna</a> of <a href="/wiki/Viti,_Kosovo" title="Viti, Kosovo">Vitina-Letnice</a>, where she often went on <a href="/wiki/Pilgrimage" title="Pilgrimage">pilgrimages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Anjezë left home in 1928 at age 18 to join the <a href="/wiki/Sisters_of_Loreto" title="Sisters of Loreto">Sisters of Loreto</a> at <a href="/wiki/Loreto_Abbey" class="mw-redirect" title="Loreto Abbey">Loreto Abbey</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rathfarnham" title="Rathfarnham">Rathfarnham</a>, Ireland, to learn English with the intent of becoming a missionary; English was the language of instruction of the Sisters of Loreto in India.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> She saw neither her mother nor her sister again.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Her family lived in Skopje until 1934, when they moved to <a href="/wiki/Tirana" title="Tirana">Tirana</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She arrived in <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a> in 1929<sup id="cite_ref-IANS2016_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IANS2016-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> and began her <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novitiate</a> in <a href="/wiki/Darjeeling" title="Darjeeling">Darjeeling</a>, in the lower <a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himalayas</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> where she learned <a href="/wiki/Bengali_language" title="Bengali language">Bengali</a> and taught at St. Teresa's School near her convent.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> She took her first <a href="/wiki/Religious_vows" title="Religious vows">religious vows</a> on 24 May 1931. She chose to be named after <a href="/wiki/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_de_Lisieux" class="mw-redirect" title="Thérèse de Lisieux">Thérèse de Lisieux</a>, the patron saint of missionaries;<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> because a nun in the convent had already chosen that name, she opted for its Spanish spelling of Teresa.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Teresa took her <a href="/wiki/Solemn_vow" title="Solemn vow">solemn vows</a> on 14 May 1937 while she was a teacher at the Loreto convent school in Entally, eastern Calcutta, taking the style of 'Mother' as part of Loreto custom.<sup id="cite_ref-MToC_14-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MToC-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-spink_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-spink-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> She served there for nearly twenty years and was appointed its headmistress in 1944.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Although Mother Teresa enjoyed teaching at the school, she was increasingly disturbed by the poverty surrounding her in <a href="/wiki/Calcutta" class="mw-redirect" title="Calcutta">Calcutta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Bengal_famine_of_1943" title="Bengal famine of 1943">Bengal famine of 1943</a> brought misery and death to the city, and the August 1946 <a href="/wiki/Direct_Action_Day" title="Direct Action Day">Direct Action Day</a> began a period of Muslim-Hindu violence.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1946, during a visit to Darjeeling by train, Mother Teresa felt that she heard the call of her inner conscience to serve the poor of India for Jesus. She asked for and received permission to leave the school. In 1950, she founded the <a href="/wiki/Missionaries_of_Charity" title="Missionaries of Charity">Missionaries of Charity</a>, choosing a white sari with two blue borders as the order's habit.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Missionaries_of_Charity">Missionaries of Charity</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Missionaries_of_Charity" title="Missionaries of Charity">Missionaries of Charity</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Three-story building with a sign and a statue" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg/220px-Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg/330px-Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg/440px-Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Missionaries_of_Charity_Mother_House.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Missionaries of Charity <a href="/wiki/Motherhouse" title="Motherhouse">motherhouse</a> in Calcutta</div></div></div>
<p>On 10 September 1946, Teresa experienced what she later described as "the call within the call" when she travelled by train to the <a href="/wiki/Loreto_Convent,_Darjeeling" title="Loreto Convent, Darjeeling">Loreto convent</a> in Darjeeling from <a href="/wiki/Calcutta" class="mw-redirect" title="Calcutta">Calcutta</a> for her annual retreat. "I was to leave the convent and help the poor while living among them. It was an order. To fail would have been to break the faith."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Joseph Langford later wrote, "Though no one knew it at the time, Sister Teresa had just become <i>Mother</i> Teresa".<sup id="cite_ref-Langford2008_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Langford2008-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She began missionary work with the poor in 1948,<sup id="cite_ref-IANS2016_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IANS2016-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> replacing her traditional Loreto <a href="/wiki/Religious_habit" title="Religious habit">habit</a> with a simple, white cotton <a href="/wiki/Sari" title="Sari">sari</a> with a blue border. Mother Teresa adopted Indian citizenship, spent several months in <a href="/wiki/Patna" title="Patna">Patna</a> to receive basic medical training at Holy Family Hospital and ventured into the slums.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> She founded a school in Motijhil, Calcutta, before she began tending to the poor and hungry.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> At the beginning of 1949, Mother Teresa was joined in her effort by a group of young women, and she laid the foundation for a new religious community helping the "poorest among the poor".<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her efforts quickly caught the attention of Indian officials, including the prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa wrote in her diary that her first year was fraught with difficulty. With no income, she begged for food and supplies and experienced doubt, loneliness and the temptation to return to the comfort of convent life during these early months:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Our Lord wants me to be a free nun covered with the poverty of the cross. Today, I learned a good lesson. The poverty of the poor must be so hard for them. While looking for a home I walked and walked till my arms and legs ached. I thought how much they must ache in body and soul, looking for a home, food and health. Then, the comfort of Loreto [her former congregation] came to tempt me. "You have only to say the word and all that will be yours again", the Tempter kept on saying.&#160;... Of free choice, my God, and out of love for you, I desire to remain and do whatever be your Holy will in my regard. I did not let a single tear come.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Sisters_of_Charity.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Four nuns in sandals and white-and-blue saris" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Sisters_of_Charity.jpg/170px-Sisters_of_Charity.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="202" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Sisters_of_Charity.jpg/255px-Sisters_of_Charity.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Sisters_of_Charity.jpg/340px-Sisters_of_Charity.jpg 2x" data-file-width="912" data-file-height="1084" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Sisters_of_Charity.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Missionaries of Charity in traditional saris</div></div></div>
<p>On 7 October 1950, Mother Teresa received <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Vatican</a> permission for the diocesan congregation, which would become the Missionaries of Charity.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In her words, it would care for "the hungry, the naked, the homeless, the crippled, the blind, the lepers, all those people who feel unwanted, unloved, uncared for throughout society, people that have become a burden to the society and are shunned by everyone".<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1952, Mother Teresa opened her first <a href="/wiki/Hospice" title="Hospice">hospice</a> with help from Calcutta officials. She converted an abandoned <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hindu</a> temple into the <a href="/wiki/Kalighat_Home_for_the_Dying" title="Kalighat Home for the Dying">Kalighat Home for the Dying</a>, free for the poor, and renamed it Kalighat, the Home of the Pure Heart (Nirmal Hriday).<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Those brought to the home received medical attention and the opportunity to <a href="/wiki/Die_with_dignity" class="mw-redirect" title="Die with dignity">die with dignity</a> in accordance with their faith: Muslims were read the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, Hindus received water from the <a href="/wiki/Ganges" title="Ganges">Ganges</a>, and Catholics received <a href="/wiki/Anointing_of_the_Sick_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Anointing of the Sick (Catholic Church)">extreme unction</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Spink55_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spink55-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> "A beautiful death", Mother Teresa said, "is for people who lived like animals to die like angels—loved and wanted."<sup id="cite_ref-Spink55_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spink55-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg" class="image"><img alt="White, older building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg/220px-Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg/330px-Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg/440px-Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="2560" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nirmal_Hriday_facade.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nirmal Hriday, Mother Teresa's Calcutta hospice, in 2007</div></div></div>
<p>She opened a hospice for those with <a href="/wiki/Leprosy" title="Leprosy">leprosy</a>, calling it Shanti Nagar (City of Peace).<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The Missionaries of Charity established leprosy-outreach clinics throughout Calcutta, providing medication, <a href="/wiki/Dressing_(medical)" title="Dressing (medical)">dressings</a> and food.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The Missionaries of Charity took in an increasing number of homeless children; in 1955, Mother Teresa opened Nirmala Shishu Bhavan, the Children's Home of the Immaculate Heart, as a haven for orphans and homeless youth.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The congregation began to attract recruits and donations, and by the 1960s it had opened hospices, orphanages and <a href="/wiki/Leper_colony" title="Leper colony">leper houses</a> throughout India. Mother Teresa then expanded the congregation abroad, opening a house in Venezuela in 1965 with five sisters.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Houses followed in Italy (Rome), Tanzania and Austria in 1968, and, during the 1970s, the congregation opened houses and foundations in the United States and dozens of countries in Asia, Africa and Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Missionaries of Charity Brothers was founded in 1963, and a <a href="/wiki/Enclosed_religious_orders" title="Enclosed religious orders">contemplative branch</a> of the Sisters followed in 1976. Lay Catholics and non-Catholics were enrolled in the Co-Workers of Mother Teresa, the Sick and Suffering Co-Workers, and the Lay Missionaries of Charity. Responding to requests by many priests, in 1981, Mother Teresa founded the Corpus Christi Movement for Priests<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> and with Joseph Langford founded the Missionaries of Charity Fathers in 1984 to combine the vocational aims of the Missionaries of Charity with the resources of the priesthood.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1997, the 13-member Calcutta congregation had grown to more than 4,000 sisters who managed orphanages, <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS" title="HIV/AIDS">AIDS</a> hospices and charity centers worldwide, caring for refugees, the blind, disabled, aged, alcoholics, the poor and homeless and victims of floods, epidemics and famine.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> By 2007, the Missionaries of Charity numbered about 450 brothers and 5,000 sisters worldwide, operating 600 missions, schools and shelters in 120 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="International_charity">International charity</span></h3>
<p>Mother Teresa said, "By blood, I am <a href="/wiki/Albania" title="Albania">Albanian</a>. By citizenship, an Indian. By faith, I am a Catholic nun. As to my calling, I belong to the world. As to my heart, I belong entirely to the Heart of Jesus."<sup id="cite_ref-Cannon2013_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cannon2013-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Fluent in five languages – <a href="/wiki/Bengali_language" title="Bengali language">Bengali</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Albanian_language" title="Albanian language">Albanian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serbian_language" title="Serbian language">Serbian</a>, <a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hindi" title="Hindi">Hindi</a> – she made occasional trips outside India for humanitarian reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the height of the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Beirut" title="Siege of Beirut">Siege of Beirut</a> in 1982, Mother Teresa rescued 37 children trapped in a front-line hospital by brokering a temporary cease-fire between the <a href="/wiki/Israel_Defense_Forces" title="Israel Defense Forces">Israeli army</a> and Palestinian guerrillas.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Accompanied by <a href="/wiki/Red_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Cross">Red Cross</a> workers, she travelled through the war zone to the hospital to evacuate the young patients.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Eastern Europe experienced increased openness in the late 1980s, Mother Teresa expanded her efforts to Communist countries which had rejected the Missionaries of Charity. She began dozens of projects, undeterred by criticism of her stands against abortion and divorce: "No matter who says what, you should accept it with a smile and do your own work." She visited <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Armenian Soviet Socialist Republic">Armenia</a> after the <a href="/wiki/1988_Armenian_earthquake" title="1988 Armenian earthquake">1988 earthquake</a><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> and met with <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Premier of the Soviet Union">Soviet Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Ryzhkov" title="Nikolai Ryzhkov">Nikolai Ryzhkov</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mother Teresa travelled to assist the hungry in Ethiopia, radiation victims at <a href="/wiki/Chernobyl" title="Chernobyl">Chernobyl</a> and earthquake victims in Armenia.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In 1991 she returned to <a href="/wiki/Albania" title="Albania">Albania</a> for the first time, opening a Missionaries of Charity Brothers home in <a href="/wiki/Tirana" title="Tirana">Tirana</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1996, the Missionaries of Charity operated 517 missions in over 100 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> The number of sisters in the Missionaries of Charity grew from twelve to thousands, serving the "poorest of the poor" in 450 centres worldwide. The first Missionaries of Charity home in the United States was established in the <a href="/wiki/South_Bronx" title="South Bronx">South Bronx</a> area of <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>, and by 1984 the congregation operated 19 establishments throughout the country.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Declining_health_and_death">Declining health and death</span></h3>
<p>Mother Teresa had a heart attack in Rome in 1983 while she was visiting <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a>. Following a second attack in 1989, she received a <a href="/wiki/Artificial_cardiac_pacemaker" title="Artificial cardiac pacemaker">pacemaker</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> In 1991, after a bout of <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a> in Mexico, she had additional heart problems. Although Mother Teresa offered to resign as head of the Missionaries of Charity, in a <a href="/wiki/Secret_ballot" title="Secret ballot">secret ballot</a> the sisters of the congregation voted for her to stay, and she agreed to continue.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1996, Mother Teresa fell, breaking her <a href="/wiki/Collarbone" class="mw-redirect" title="Collarbone">collarbone</a>, and four months later she had <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Left_heart_failure" class="mw-redirect" title="Left heart failure">heart failure</a>. Although she underwent <a href="/wiki/Cardiac_surgery" title="Cardiac surgery">heart surgery</a>, her health was clearly declining. According to Archbishop of Calcutta <a href="/wiki/Henry_Sebastian_D%27Souza" title="Henry Sebastian D&#39;Souza">Henry Sebastian D'Souza</a>, he ordered a priest to perform an <a href="/wiki/Exorcism" title="Exorcism">exorcism</a> (with her permission) when she was first hospitalised with cardiac problems because he thought she might be under attack by <a href="/wiki/Devil_in_Christianity" title="Devil in Christianity">the devil</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 13 March 1997, Mother Teresa resigned as head of the Missionaries of Charity. She died on 5 September.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> At the time of her death, the Missionaries of Charity had over 4,000 sisters and an associated brotherhood of 300 members operating 610 missions in 123 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> These included hospices and homes for people with HIV/AIDS, leprosy and tuberculosis, soup kitchens, children's and family counselling programmes, orphanages and schools. The Missionaries of Charity were aided by co-workers numbering over one million by the 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mother Teresa <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_repose" title="Lying in repose">lay in repose</a> in an open casket in <a href="/wiki/St._Thomas_Church,_Kolkata" title="St. Thomas Church, Kolkata">St Thomas, Calcutta</a>, for a week before her funeral. She received a <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state funeral</a> from the Indian government in gratitude for her service to the poor of all religions in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Secretary_of_State" title="Cardinal Secretary of State">Cardinal Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Angelo_Sodano" title="Angelo Sodano">Angelo Sodano</a>, the Pope's representative, delivered the homily at the service.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa's death was mourned in the secular and religious communities. <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Pakistan" title="Prime Minister of Pakistan">Prime Minister of Pakistan</a> <a href="/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif" title="Nawaz Sharif">Nawaz Sharif</a> called her "a rare and unique individual who lived long for higher purposes. Her life-long devotion to the care of the poor, the sick, and the disadvantaged was one of the highest examples of service to our humanity."<sup id="cite_ref-:2_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> According to former <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">U.N. Secretary-General</a> <a href="/wiki/Javier_P%C3%A9rez_de_Cu%C3%A9llar" title="Javier Pérez de Cuéllar">Javier Pérez de Cuéllar</a>, "She is the United Nations. She is peace in the world."<sup id="cite_ref-:2_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Recognition_and_reception">Recognition and reception</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="India">India</span></h3>
<p>From the Indian government, under the name of Mary Teresa Bojaxhiu<b>,</b> Mother Teresa was issued a diplomatic passport.<sup id="cite_ref-Navin_Chawla_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Navin_Chawla-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>  She received the <a href="/wiki/Padma_Shri" title="Padma Shri">Padma Shri</a> in 1962 and the <a href="/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru_Award_for_International_Understanding" class="mw-redirect" title="Jawaharlal Nehru Award for International Understanding">Jawaharlal Nehru Award for International Understanding</a> in 1969.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> She later received other Indian awards, including the <a href="/wiki/Bharat_Ratna" title="Bharat Ratna">Bharat Ratna</a> (India's highest civilian award) in 1980.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa's official biography, by <a href="/wiki/Navin_Chawla" title="Navin Chawla">Navin Chawla</a>, was published in 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> In Calcutta, she is worshipped as a deity by some <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hindus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>To commemorate the 100th anniversary of her birth, the government of India issued a special <span style="white-space: nowrap">₹</span>5 coin (the amount of money Mother Teresa had when she arrived in India) on 28 August 2010. President <a href="/wiki/Pratibha_Patil" title="Pratibha Patil">Pratibha Patil</a> said, "Clad in a white sari with a blue border, she and the sisters of Missionaries of Charity became a symbol of hope to many—namely, the aged, the destitute, the unemployed, the diseased, the terminally ill, and those abandoned by their families."<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Indian views of Mother Teresa are not uniformly favourable. <a href="/wiki/Aroup_Chatterjee" title="Aroup Chatterjee">Aroup Chatterjee</a>, a physician born and raised in Calcutta who was an activist in the city's slums for years around 1980 before moving to the UK, said that he "never even saw any nuns in those slums".<sup id="cite_ref-:4_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> His research, involving more than 100 interviews with volunteers, nuns and others familiar with the Missionaries of Charity, was described in a 2003 book critical of Mother Teresa.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_87-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Chatterjee criticized her for promoting a "cult of suffering" and a distorted, negative image of Calcutta, exaggerating work done by her mission and misusing funds and privileges at her disposal.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_87-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Chatterjee_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chatterjee-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> According to him, some of the hygiene problems he had criticized (such as the reuse of <a href="/wiki/Hypodermic_needles" class="mw-redirect" title="Hypodermic needles">needles</a>) improved after Mother Teresa's death in 1997.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_87-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Bikash_Ranjan_Bhattacharya" title="Bikash Ranjan Bhattacharya">Bikash Ranjan Bhattacharya</a>, mayor of Calcutta from 2005 to 2010, said that "she had no significant impact on the poor of this city", glorified illness instead of treating it and misrepresented the city: "No doubt there was poverty in Calcutta, but it was never a city of lepers and beggars, as Mother Teresa presented it."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> On the <a href="/wiki/Hindu_nationalism" title="Hindu nationalism">Hindu right</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party" title="Bharatiya Janata Party">Bharatiya Janata Party</a> clashed with Mother Teresa over the Christian <a href="/wiki/Dalit" title="Dalit">Dalits</a> but praised her in death and sent a representative to her funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Vishwa_Hindu_Parishad" class="mw-redirect" title="Vishwa Hindu Parishad">Vishwa Hindu Parishad</a>, however, opposed the government decision to grant her a state funeral. Secretary <a href="/wiki/Giriraj_Kishore" title="Giriraj Kishore">Giriraj Kishore</a> said that "her first duty was to the Church and social service was incidental", accusing her of favouring Christians and conducting "secret baptisms" of the dying.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-usislam1_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-usislam1-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In a front-page tribute, the Indian fortnightly <i><a href="/wiki/Frontline_(magazine)" title="Frontline (magazine)">Frontline</a></i> dismissed the charges as "patently false" and said that they had "made no impact on the public perception of her work, especially in Calcutta". Praising her "selfless caring", energy and bravery, the author of the tribute criticised Teresa's public campaign against abortion and her claim to be non-political.<sup id="cite_ref-Frontline_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frontline-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2015 <a href="/wiki/Mohan_Bhagwat" title="Mohan Bhagwat">Mohan Bhagwat</a>, leader of the Hindu right-wing organisation <a href="/wiki/Rashtriya_Swayamsevak_Sangh" title="Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh">Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh</a>, said that Mother Teresa's objective was "to convert the person, who was being served, into a Christian".<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Former RSS spokesperson M. G. Vaidhya supported Bhagwat's assessment, and the organisation accused the media of "distorting facts about Bhagwat's remarks". <a href="/wiki/Trinamool_Congress" title="Trinamool Congress">Trinamool Congress</a> MP <a href="/wiki/Derek_O%27Brien_(quizmaster)" class="mw-redirect" title="Derek O&#39;Brien (quizmaster)">Derek O'Brien</a>, <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India" title="Communist Party of India">CPI</a> leader Atul Anjan and <a href="/wiki/Delhi" title="Delhi">Delhi</a> chief minister <a href="/wiki/Arvind_Kejriwal" title="Arvind Kejriwal">Arvind Kejriwal</a> protested Bhagwat's statement.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>  In 1991<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> the country's first modern University, <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Serampore_College_(University)" title="Senate of Serampore College (University)">Senate of Serampore College (University)</a> awarded a honorary doctorate during registrarship of <a href="/wiki/D._S._Satyaranjan" title="D. S. Satyaranjan">D. S. Satyaranjan</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Elsewhere">Elsewhere</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg" class="image"><img alt="US President Ronald Regan and Nancy Reagan with Mother Teresa, standing at a microphone" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg/220px-President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg/330px-President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg/440px-President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3150" data-file-height="2078" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_Mother_Teresa_with_the_Medal_of_Freedom_at_a_White_House_Ceremony_in_the_Rose_Garden.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">President</a> <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Ronald Reagan</a> presents Mother Teresa with the <a href="/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom" title="Presidential Medal of Freedom">Presidential Medal of Freedom</a> at a White House ceremony as First Lady <a href="/wiki/Nancy_Reagan" title="Nancy Reagan">Nancy Reagan</a> looks on, 20 June 1985.</div></div></div>
<p>Mother Teresa received the <a href="/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay_Award" title="Ramon Magsaysay Award">Ramon Magsaysay Award</a> for Peace and International Understanding, given for work in South or East Asia, in 1962. According to its citation, "The Board of Trustees recognises her merciful cognisance of the abject poor of a foreign land, in whose service she has led a new congregation".<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> By the early 1970s, Mother Teresa was an international celebrity. She had been catapulted to fame via <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Muggeridge" title="Malcolm Muggeridge">Malcolm Muggeridge</a>'s 1969 <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a> documentary, <i>Something Beautiful for God</i>, before he released a <a href="/wiki/Something_Beautiful_for_God" title="Something Beautiful for God">1971 book of the same name</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Muggeridge was undergoing a spiritual journey of his own at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Timecrisis_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timecrisis-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> During filming, footage shot in poor lighting (particularly at the Home for the Dying) was thought unlikely to be usable by the crew; the crew had been using new, untested <a href="/wiki/Photographic_film" title="Photographic film">photographic film</a>. In England, the footage was found to be extremely well-lit and Muggeridge called it a miracle of "divine light" from Teresa.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Other crew members said that it was due to a new type of ultra-sensitive Kodak film.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Muggeridge later converted to Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Around this time, the Catholic world began to honour Mother Teresa publicly. Pope <a href="/wiki/Paul_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Paul VI">Paul VI</a> gave her the inaugural <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII" title="Pope John XXIII">Pope John XXIII</a> Peace Prize in 1971, commending her work with the poor, her display of Christian charity and her efforts for peace.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> She received the <a href="/wiki/Pacem_in_Terris_Award" title="Pacem in Terris Award">Pacem in Terris Award</a> in 1976.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> After her death, Teresa progressed rapidly on the road to <a href="/wiki/Sainthood" class="mw-redirect" title="Sainthood">sainthood</a>.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg/220px-Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="222" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg/330px-Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/db/Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg 2x" data-file-width="371" data-file-height="375" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mother_Teresa_%26_Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Mother Teresa with <a href="/wiki/Mich%C3%A8le_Duvalier" class="mw-redirect" title="Michèle Duvalier">Michèle Duvalier</a> in January 1981.</div></div></div>
<p>She was honoured by governments and civilian organisations and appointed an honorary Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Order of Australia</a> in 1982 "for service to the community of Australia and humanity at large".<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The United Kingdom and the United States bestowed a number of awards, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Merit" title="Order of Merit">Order of Merit</a> in 1983 and <a href="/wiki/Honorary_citizenship_of_the_United_States" title="Honorary citizenship of the United States">honorary citizenship of the United States</a> on 16 November 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa's Albanian homeland gave her the Golden Honour of the Nation in 1994,<sup id="cite_ref-Frontline_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frontline-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> but her acceptance of this and the Haitian Legion of Honour was controversial. Mother Teresa was criticised for implicitly supporting the <a href="/wiki/Duvalier" title="Duvalier">Duvaliers</a> and corrupt businessmen such as <a href="/wiki/Charles_Keating" title="Charles Keating">Charles Keating</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Maxwell" title="Robert Maxwell">Robert Maxwell</a>; she wrote to the judge of Keating's trial requesting clemency.<sup id="cite_ref-Frontline_93-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frontline-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BMJ_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BMJ-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Universities in India and the West granted her honorary degrees.<sup id="cite_ref-Frontline_93-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Frontline-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Other civilian awards included the <a href="/wiki/Balzan_Prize" title="Balzan Prize">Balzan Prize</a> for promoting humanity, peace and brotherhood among peoples (1978)<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Albert_Schweitzer" title="Albert Schweitzer">Albert Schweitzer</a> International Prize (1975).<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In April 1976, Mother Teresa visited the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Scranton" title="University of Scranton">University of Scranton</a> in northeastern <a href="/wiki/Pennsylvania" title="Pennsylvania">Pennsylvania</a>, where she received the La Storta Medal for Human Service from university president <a href="/wiki/William_J._Byron" title="William J. Byron">William J. Byron</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Mother_Teresa_Addresses_4,500_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mother_Teresa_Addresses_4,500-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> She challenged an audience of 4,500 to "know poor people in your own home and local neighbourhood", feeding others or simply spreading joy and love.<sup id="cite_ref-Spread_Love,_Help_Poor_of_Heart_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spread_Love,_Help_Poor_of_Heart-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa continued: "The poor will help us grow in sanctity, for they are Christ in the guise of distress".<sup id="cite_ref-Mother_Teresa_Addresses_4,500_110-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mother_Teresa_Addresses_4,500-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> In August 1987, Mother Teresa received an honorary doctor of social science degree from the university in recognition of her service and her ministry to help the destitute and sick.<sup id="cite_ref-U_of_S_Honorary_Degree_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-U_of_S_Honorary_Degree-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> She spoke to over 4,000 students and members of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Scranton" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Scranton">Diocese of Scranton</a><sup id="cite_ref-MT_in_Scranton_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MT_in_Scranton-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> about her service to the "poorest of the poor", telling them to "do small things with great love".<sup id="cite_ref-Small_Things_with_Great_Love_Address_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Small_Things_with_Great_Love_Address-114">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During her lifetime, Mother Teresa was among the top 10 women in the annual <a href="/wiki/Gallup%27s_most_admired_man_and_woman_poll" title="Gallup&#39;s most admired man and woman poll">Gallup's most admired man and woman poll</a> 18 times, finishing first several times in the 1980s and 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> In 1999 she headed <a href="/wiki/Gallup%27s_List_of_Most_Widely_Admired_People_of_the_20th_Century" title="Gallup&#39;s List of Most Widely Admired People of the 20th Century">Gallup's List of Most Widely Admired People of the 20th Century</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-gallup-20c_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gallup-20c-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> out-polling all other volunteered answers by a wide margin. She was first in all major demographic categories except the very young.<sup id="cite_ref-gallup-20c_116-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gallup-20c-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Nobel_Peace_Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox" style="width: 210px; float: right; clear: right; margin:0 0 1.5em 1.5em"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:115%">External video</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="text-align: left"><img alt="video icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/16px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/24px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/32px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="128" data-file-height="128" /> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.nobelprize.org/mediaplayer/index.php?id=1852">Mother Teresa's 1979 Nobel Peace Prize acceptance speech</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In 1979, Mother Teresa received the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> "for work undertaken in the struggle to overcome poverty and distress, which also constitutes a threat to peace".<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> She refused the conventional ceremonial banquet for laureates, asking that its $192,000 cost be given to the poor in India<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> and saying that earthly rewards were important only if they helped her to help the world's needy. When Mother Teresa received the prize she was asked, "What can we do to promote world peace?" She answered, "Go home and love your family." Building on this theme in her <a href="/wiki/Nobel_lecture" class="mw-redirect" title="Nobel lecture">Nobel lecture</a>, she said: "Around the world, not only in the poor countries, but I found the poverty of the West so much more difficult to remove. When I pick up a person from the street, hungry, I give him a plate of rice, a piece of bread, I have satisfied. I have removed that hunger. But a person that is shut out, that feels unwanted, unloved, terrified, the person that has been thrown out from society – that poverty is so hurtable &#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; and so much, and I find that very difficult."
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_and_political_views">Social and political views</span></h2>
<p>Mother Teresa singled out abortion as "the greatest destroyer of peace today. Because if a mother can kill her own child – what is left for me to kill you and you kill me – there is nothing between."<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Barbara Smoker of the <a href="/wiki/Secular_humanism" title="Secular humanism">secular humanist</a> magazine <i><a href="/wiki/The_Freethinker_(journal)" title="The Freethinker (journal)">The Freethinker</a></i> criticised Mother Teresa after the Peace Prize award, saying that her promotion of Catholic moral teachings on abortion and contraception diverted funds from effective methods to solve India's problems.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_World_Conference_on_Women" class="mw-redirect" title="Fourth World Conference on Women">Fourth World Conference on Women</a> in <a href="/wiki/Beijing" title="Beijing">Beijing</a>, Mother Teresa said: "Yet we can destroy this gift of motherhood, especially by the evil of abortion, but also by thinking that other things like jobs or positions are more important than loving."<sup id="cite_ref-Mother_Teresa&#39;s_Message_to_Fourth_UN_Women&#39;s_Conference_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mother_Teresa&#39;s_Message_to_Fourth_UN_Women&#39;s_Conference-122">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Criticism">Criticism</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Criticism_of_Mother_Teresa" title="Criticism of Mother Teresa">Criticism of Mother Teresa</a></div>
<p>According to a paper by Canadian academics Serge Larivée, Geneviève Chénard and Carole Sénéchal, Mother Teresa's clinics received millions of dollars in donations but lacked <a href="/wiki/Health_care" title="Health care">medical care</a>, systematic diagnosis, necessary nutrition and sufficient <a href="/wiki/Analgesic" title="Analgesic">analgesics</a> for those in pain;<sup id="cite_ref-Université_de_Montréal_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Université_de_Montréal-123">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> in the opinion of the three academics, "Mother Teresa believed the sick must suffer like Christ on the cross".<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> It was said that the additional money might have transformed the health of the city's poor by creating advanced <a href="/wiki/Palliative_care" title="Palliative care">palliative care</a> facilities.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of Mother Teresa's most outspoken critics was English journalist and <a href="/wiki/Antitheism" title="Antitheism">antitheist</a> <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Hitchens" title="Christopher Hitchens">Christopher Hitchens</a>, host of the documentary <i><a href="/wiki/Hell%27s_Angel_(documentary)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hell&#39;s Angel (documentary)">Hell's Angel</a></i> (1994) and author of the essay <i><a href="/wiki/The_Missionary_Position:_Mother_Teresa_in_Theory_and_Practice" title="The Missionary Position: Mother Teresa in Theory and Practice">The Missionary Position: Mother Teresa in Theory and Practice</a></i> (1995) who wrote in a 2003 article: "This returns us to the medieval corruption of the church, which sold indulgences to the rich while preaching hellfire and continence to the poor. [Mother Teresa] was not a friend of the poor. She was a friend of <em>poverty</em>. She said that suffering was a gift from God. She spent her life opposing the only known cure for poverty, which is the empowerment of women and the emancipation of them from a livestock version of compulsory reproduction."<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> He accused her of <a href="/wiki/Hypocrisy" title="Hypocrisy">hypocrisy</a> for choosing advanced treatment for her heart condition.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Hitchens said that "her intention was not to help people", and that she lied to donors about how their contributions were used. "It was by talking to her that I discovered, and she assured me, that she wasn't working to alleviate poverty", he said, "She was working to expand the number of Catholics. She said, 'I'm not a social worker. I don't do it for this reason. I do it for Christ. I do it for the church<span style="padding-right:.15em;">'</span>".<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Although Hitchens thought he was the only witness called by the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>, <a href="/wiki/Aroup_Chatterjee" title="Aroup Chatterjee">Aroup Chatterjee</a> (author of <i>Mother Teresa: The Untold Story</i>) was also called to present evidence opposing Mother Teresa's beatification and canonisation.<sup id="cite_ref-Hitchens_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hitchens-131">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1994, Mother Teresa argued that the sexual abuse allegations against Jesuit priest <a href="/wiki/Donald_McGuire_(Jesuit)" title="Donald McGuire (Jesuit)">Donald McGuire</a> were untrue. When he was convicted of sexually molesting multiple children in 2006, Mother Teresa's defense of him was criticised.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements" title="Abortion-rights movements">Abortion-rights groups</a> have also criticised Mother Teresa's stance against abortion and contraception.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Spiritual_life">Spiritual life</span></h2>
<p>Analysing her deeds and achievements, <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> said: "Where did Mother Teresa find the strength and perseverance to place herself completely at the service of others? She found it in prayer and in the silent contemplation of Jesus Christ, his Holy Face, his Sacred Heart."<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Privately, Mother Teresa experienced doubts and struggle in her religious beliefs which lasted nearly 50 years, until the end of her life.<sup id="cite_ref-TIME-2007-08-23_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TIME-2007-08-23-138">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa expressed grave doubts about God's existence and pain over her lack of faith:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Where is my faith? Even deep down<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>[...] there is nothing but emptiness and darkness.<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>[...] If there be God – please forgive me. When I try to raise my thoughts to Heaven, there is such convicting emptiness that those very thoughts return like sharp knives and hurt my very soul.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Outdoor bas-relief plaque" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg/220px-Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg/330px-Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg/440px-Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1548" data-file-height="1548" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mother_Teresa_memorial_plaque.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Plaque dedicated to Mother Teresa in Wenceslas Square, <a href="/wiki/Olomouc" title="Olomouc">Olomouc</a>, Czech Republic</div></div></div>
<p>Other saints (including Teresa's namesake <a href="/wiki/Th%C3%A9r%C3%A8se_of_Lisieux" title="Thérèse of Lisieux">Thérèse of Lisieux</a>, who called it a "night of nothingness") had similar experiences of <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_dryness" title="Spiritual dryness">spiritual dryness</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-140">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> According to James Langford, these doubts were typical and would not be an impediment to canonisation.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_140-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-140">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After ten years of doubt, Mother Teresa described a brief period of renewed faith. After <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>'s death in 1958, she was praying for him at a requiem mass when she was relieved of "the long darkness: that strange suffering." However, five weeks later her spiritual dryness returned.<sup id="cite_ref-Newsweekteresa_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newsweekteresa-141">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mother Teresa wrote many letters to her confessors and superiors over a 66-year period, most notably to Calcutta Archbishop Ferdinand Perier and Jesuit priest <a href="/wiki/Celeste_van_Exem" title="Celeste van Exem">Celeste van Exem</a> (her spiritual advisor since the formation of the Missionaries of Charity).<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> She requested that her letters be destroyed, concerned that "people will think more of me – less of Jesus."<sup id="cite_ref-Timecrisis_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timecrisis-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG/220px-Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="260" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG/330px-Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG/440px-Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG 2x" data-file-width="929" data-file-height="1098" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfons_Weisser_Mutter_Teresa.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Semi-abstract painting honouring Mother Teresa</div></div></div>
<p>However, the correspondence was compiled in <i>Mother Teresa: Come Be My Light</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Timecrisis_99-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timecrisis-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Kolodiejchuk_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kolodiejchuk-144">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Mother Teresa wrote to spiritual confidant Michael van der Peet, "Jesus has a very special love for you. [But] as for me, the silence and the emptiness is so great, that I look and do not see – listen and do not hear – the tongue moves [in prayer] but does not speak.<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>[...] I want you to pray for me – that I let Him have [a] free hand."
</p><p>In <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Deus_caritas_est" title="Deus caritas est">Deus caritas est</a></i></span> (his first <a href="/wiki/Encyclical" title="Encyclical">encyclical</a>), <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Pope Benedict XVI">Pope Benedict XVI</a> mentioned Mother Teresa three times and used her life to clarify one of the encyclical's main points: "In the example of Blessed Teresa of Calcutta we have a clear illustration of the fact that time devoted to God in prayer not only does not detract from effective and loving service to our neighbour but is in fact the inexhaustible source of that service."<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> She wrote, "It is only by <a href="/wiki/Mental_prayer" title="Mental prayer">mental prayer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Spiritual_reading" title="Spiritual reading">spiritual reading</a> that we can cultivate the gift of prayer."<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although her order was not connected with the Franciscan orders, Mother Teresa admired <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a><sup id="cite_ref-AmeriCath_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AmeriCath-147">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> and was influenced by Franciscan spirituality. The Sisters of Charity recite the <a href="/wiki/Prayer_of_Saint_Francis" title="Prayer of Saint Francis">prayer of Saint Francis</a> every morning at Mass during the <a href="/wiki/Thanksgiving_after_Communion" title="Thanksgiving after Communion">thanksgiving after Communion</a>, and their emphasis on ministry and many of their vows are similar.<sup id="cite_ref-AmeriCath_147-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AmeriCath-147">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Francis emphasised poverty, chastity, obedience and submission to Christ. He devoted much of his life to serving the poor, particularly lepers.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonization">Canonization</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Miracle_and_beatification">Miracle and beatification</span></h3>
<p>After Mother Teresa's death in 1997, the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a> began the process of <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> (the second of three steps towards <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonization</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Brian_Kolodiejchuk" title="Brian Kolodiejchuk">Brian Kolodiejchuk</a> was appointed <a href="/wiki/Postulator" title="Postulator">postulator</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Calcutta" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Calcutta">Diocese of Calcutta</a>. Although he said, "We didn't have to prove that she was perfect or never made a mistake", he had to prove that Mother Teresa's virtue was heroic. Kolodiejchuk submitted 76 documents, totalling 35,000 pages, which were based on interviews with 113 witnesses who were asked to answer 263 questions.<sup id="cite_ref-Brown_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brown-149">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:162px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg/160px-Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg" decoding="async" width="160" height="332" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg/240px-Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg/320px-Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1263" data-file-height="2618" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stained_glass_depiction_of_key_moments_in_the_lifetime_of_Mother_Teresa_at_the_Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa_in_Prishtina.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stained glass depiction of key moments in the lifetime of Mother Teresa at the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_Saint_Mother_Teresa,_Pristina" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral of Saint Mother Teresa, Pristina">Cathedral of Saint Mother Teresa</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prishtina" class="mw-redirect" title="Prishtina">Prishtina</a>, Kosovo</div></div></div>
<p>The process of canonisation requires the documentation of a <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miracle</a> resulting from the <a href="/wiki/Intercession" title="Intercession">intercession</a> of the prospective saint.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> In 2002 the Vatican recognised as a miracle the healing of a tumour in the abdomen of Monica Besra, an Indian woman, after the application of a locket containing Teresa's picture. According to Besra, a beam of light emanated from the picture and her cancerous tumour was cured; however, her husband and some of her medical staff said that conventional medical treatment eradicated the tumour.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Ranjan Mustafi, who told <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Times" title="The New York Times">The New York Times</a></i> he had treated Besra, said that the cyst was caused by tuberculosis: "It was not a miracle&#160;... She took medicines for nine months to one year."<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> According to Besra's husband, "My wife was cured by the doctors and not by any miracle [...] This miracle is a hoax."<sup id="cite_ref-time_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time-153">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Besra said that her medical records, including sonograms, prescriptions and physicians' notes, were confiscated by Sister Betta of the Missionaries of Charity. According to <i><a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)">Time</a></i>, calls to Sister Betta and the office of Sister Nirmala (Teresa's successor as head of the order) produced no comment. Officials at Balurghat Hospital, where Besra sought medical treatment, said that they were pressured by the order to call her cure miraculous.<sup id="cite_ref-time_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time-153">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> In February 2000, former West Bengal health minister Partho De ordered a review of Besra's medical records at the Department of Health in Calcutta. According to De, there was nothing unusual about her illness and cure based on her lengthy treatment. He said that he had refused to give the Vatican the name of a doctor who would certify that Monica Besra's healing was a miracle.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Mother Teresa's beatification and canonisation, the Vatican studied published and unpublished criticism of her life and work.
